--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1:**

The champion (score 4684.71) uses:
- State 0: GoAwayColor (color 1=red), moves away from red → keeps moving in phase 1
- State 1: Stop → stationary in phase 2
- Transitions from state 0: two probabilistic transitions to state 1 (p=0.549 and p=0.071)
- Transitions from state 1: back to state 0 with low probability

The key insight is that the champion uses GoAwayColor with color 1 (red) to keep moving, then probabilistically transitions to Stop. However, the mission requires detecting the GREEN block (color 2) to trigger the stop. The champion's transitions are purely probabilistic, which is suboptimal.

**What we need:**
- A moving state (phase 1): exploration, attraction, repulsion, or GoAwayColor
- A stopped state (phase 2): Stop behavior
- A transition from moving→stopped triggered by **perceiving green (color 2)** with high probability
- The stop state should resist going back to moving

**Strategy:**
1. Keep the champion verbatim (elitism)
2. Exploit: modify champion with green-perception trigger (C=7, color 2) from moving state
3. Explore: new topologies with explicit green detection

The champion's moving→stop is purely probabilistic. We should add a C=7 (perceive color) condition with color 2 (green) to trigger the stop. Let me design better machines.

**Key designs:**
- 2-state: Exploration/GoAwayColor → Stop, with C=7 (green, color 2) as main trigger + low prob fallback
- 3-state: Exploration → GoToColor(green) → Stop, chaining detection and approach then stop
- The stop state needs very low probability of returning to moving

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 2:**

The champion (score 2824.68) is:
- State 0: GoAwayColor(red/color1) → keeps moving away from red walls during phase 1
- Transition 0→1: C=7 (perceive green/color2) with p=0.95 → triggered when green block appears
- Transition 0→1: C=5 (fixed prob) p=0.002 → tiny fallback
- State 1: Stop → stays still during phase 2
- Transition 1→0: C=5 p=0.001 → almost never returns

This is a strong design. The key mechanism: GoAwayColor(red) keeps robots moving while bouncing off red walls, and when green appears, C=7 triggers the stop with 95% probability per step.

**Exploitation directions:**
1. Increase green-detection probability (0.95 → 0.99) to react faster
2. Reduce fallback probability even further (0.002 → 0.0005)
3. Try different moving behaviors (Exploration, Repulsion) with same trigger
4. Add a secondary green-detection condition to stop state (prevent returning)
5. Try p=1.0 for green detection (maximum responsiveness)

**Why the champion beats others:**
- GoAwayColor(red) is good because red walls are always present, so robots always have something to move away from → good coverage/movement
- The green detection is the crucial trigger

**New ideas:**
- What if stop state also detects green and reinforces staying? Add C=7(green) self-reinforcing... but can't point to own state
- Add 3-state: moving → intermediate(GoToColor green to cluster near green block?) → stop
- Try GoAwayColor with color 2 (green) as mover... no that would move away from green when it appears
- Try Repulsion as mover for more dispersal
- Increase p for green detection to 0.99 or 1.0

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 3:**

The champion (2640.54) is:
- State 0: GoAwayColor(red/color1), transitions on green-perception (p=0.95) or tiny prob (p=0.001) → Stop
- State 1: Stop, tiny prob (p=0.0005) back to moving

Key findings:
1. GoAwayColor(red) is the best moving behavior - keeps robots active near walls
2. Green detection (C=7, color 2) with p=0.95 is the key trigger
3. The 3-state variant (rank 2, score 2645.46) with a GoToColor(green) intermediate state is almost as good
4. p=0.95 slightly beats p=0.99 for green detection - perhaps p=0.99 is too eager (false positives before green appears?)
5. Lower fallback probability (0.0005) in stop state helps

**Key question:** Why does p=0.95 beat p=0.99? The color condition fires based on camera perception. If p=0.99 vs p=0.95 makes little difference in stopping speed but p=0.95 allows slightly more movement in phase 1 (if there's any pre-green false detection of color 2)... Actually the arena has no green initially, so C=7(green) should never fire in phase 1. The difference must be noise in simulation.

**Exploitation strategy:**
1. Try p values between 0.95 and 0.99 (0.96, 0.97)
2. Try p=0.001 fallback vs p=0.0005 vs p=0.0002
3. Explore the 3-state topology from rank 2 more carefully
4. Try making stop state have NO return transition (only 1 state)... wait, must have n>=1 transitions

**New ideas:**
- Try p=0.0001 for return from stop (even more stable)
- Try the 3-state with GoAwayColor(red) → Stop with green detection, and a GoToColor(green) recovery state
- Vary the fallback probability in state 0 (currently 0.001, try 0.0005)

# ROUND 5/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 4:**

The champion (2394.71) has:
- State 0: GoAwayColor(red), C=7(green, p=0.95) → Stop, C=5(p=0.0005) → Stop
- State 1: Stop, C=5(p=0.0002) → GoAwayColor(red)

Key findings:
1. Lower fallback probabilities (0.0005 for moving→stop, 0.0002 for stop→moving) are better
2. p=0.95 vs p=0.99 for green detection are very close (2394 vs 2405)
3. The 3-state variant with GoToColor(green) as intermediate is close (2423)
4. Higher fallback (0.002) in moving state is worse

**What's the bottleneck?** The score of ~2394 means roughly 2394 robot-steps of penalty. With 10 robots and ~500 steps in phase 1, if all move perfectly that's 0 phase-1 cost, plus ~700 steps in phase 2 if they stop at step ~500. If they stop within 1-2 steps of green appearing (95% chance each step), the average delay is ~1/0.95 ≈ 1.05 steps, so 10 robots × 1 step ≈ 10 steps penalty from stopping delay. The remaining ~2384 must come from phase 2 leakage (robots waking up from stop) or phase 1 leakage (robots stopping before green).

Phase 1 leakage: p=0.0005 per step × ~500 steps × 10 robots = 2.5 steps of unintended stopping - minimal.
Phase 2 leakage: p=0.0002 per step × ~700 steps × 10 robots = 1.4 steps of unintended moving - minimal.

The bulk of cost (~2394) must be from phase 2 time AFTER green appears but before detection. Wait - the score gains +1 for every robot MOVING in phase 2. So if robots stop quickly (within 1-2 steps), the phase 2 cost should be small. Unless some robots never see the green block from their position...

**Key insight:** Not all robots may perceive the green block! If a robot is facing away or too far, C=7 might not fire. The 0.0005 fallback in state 0 means the robot will eventually stop, but at random. This explains why scores aren't lower.

**Strategy:** Perhaps we need MULTIPLE green-detection attempts (more transitions) or combine with a behavior that moves robots toward green. The 3-state with GoToColor(green) intermediate might help robots get close enough to see green.

**Exploitation:**
- Try p=0.0001 for return from stop
- Try very tiny fallback (1e-4) for moving→stop
- Try 3-state with better parameters
- Add a second green-detection transition from stop state (to prevent false returns)

**Exploration:**
- 3-state: GoAwayRed → GoToGreen → Stop (chain that guides robots toward green)
- 4-state design

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 5:**

The champion (2394.71) held its position. The best performers this round were mostly variations of the champion but slightly worse. Key observations:

1. The champion structure is very solid: GoAwayColor(red) + green detection (p=0.95) + tiny fallback (0.0005) → Stop with minimal return probability (0.0002)
2. The double green-detection transition (rank 1, 2508) didn't beat the champion
3. 3-state designs with GoToColor(green) intermediate are consistently worse than 2-state
4. The stop state LED color (cyan=6 vs yellow=4) doesn't matter much (both score ~2513)

**The problem:** The champion score of ~2394 suggests there's still room for improvement. The theoretical minimum is close to zero (if robots stop instantly when green appears and never move again). Let me think about what's causing the residual cost:

- Phase 1: With p=0.0005 fallback, about 0.0005 × 500 steps × 10 robots = 2.5 penalty units - negligible
- Phase 2: With p=0.0002 return rate, about 0.0002 × 700 steps × 10 robots = 1.4 penalty units - negligible
- The main cost (~2394) must be from robots that DON'T see the green block immediately when it appears

The key issue: GoAwayColor(red) keeps robots near walls (bouncing off red walls), but the green block is on ONE randomly chosen wall. Robots near the green wall will see it; robots on the other side might not. With 10 robots in a small arena (r=0.9m), most should be able to see 0.45m range... but the arena is 0.9m inner radius, so diameter is 1.8m, much larger than the 0.45m range. Robots far from the green wall won't detect it.

**What helps:** Robots that spread out more (Exploration/random walk) might cover the arena better, but GoAwayColor(red) pushes them to walls. Actually, GoAwayColor(red) means robots face away from walls and move toward center... wait, no - GoAway means move AWAY from the red object, so robots move TOWARD the center. This keeps them spread out.

Actually, if robots are moving toward center (away from red walls), they stay in the middle where they might not see any wall clearly. When green appears, they need to be close enough to detect it.

**New strategy ideas:**
- Try GoToColor(red) as the mover - keeps robots near walls, closer to where green will appear
- Try Exploration (random walk) - disperses robots randomly, some will be near green wall
- Try with p=0.95 AND a secondary check - but that didn't help

Let me try GoToColor(red) as the phase 1 mover - robots cluster near walls, maximizing chance of being near the green wall when it appears.

Also try reducing p further (0.0001 return from stop).

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 6:**

The champion (2394.71) still holds. Key observations:

1. GoToColor(red)/B=8 with p=0.95 green detection scored 2520 - interesting! GoToColor(red) means robots move TOWARD red walls, clustering near walls where the green block will appear. This could help detection.
2. Exploration (random walk) is bad - scores 3693-4569. Random walk doesn't keep robots near walls.
3. Double green detection (two C=7 transitions) didn't help much.
4. The champion's GoAwayColor(red) keeps robots active and spread somewhat.

**Key insight:** GoToColor(red) (B=8, clr=1) scored 2520 with p=1e-4 return from stop - close to champion. Robots moving TOWARD red walls might actually see green better since they're near the walls. Let me explore this more.

**What's the irreducible cost?** With 10 robots, ~500 phase-1 steps, and only 1/8 walls turning green, a robot near a non-green wall might not detect green at all with 0.45m range. Average detection delay = sum of geometric series = roughly 1/(p_detect) where p_detect depends on proximity and orientation. The fallback (0.0005) gives average 2000 steps before stopping for robots that never see green - but that's way too long.

**Critical realization:** If some robots never see green, they rely on the p=0.0005 fallback → average 2000 steps before stopping → contributes enormously to score. We need the fallback to be higher for robots far from green, but that causes false stops in phase 1.

**Alternative approach:** What if we use a HIGHER fallback (like 0.01) but compensate with green detection? The expected phase-1 cost from false stops: 0.001 × 500 × 10 = 5 steps. The phase-2 cost from delayed stopping for blind robots: 1/0.01 × 10 robots × fraction_blind. If 2/8 walls have robots that can't see green = 2.5 robots blind → 2.5 × 100 steps = 250. This might be better than 2394!

Wait, let me reconsider. The champion score of 2394 with 10 robots over 1200 steps... if it worked perfectly we'd expect close to 0. The score must include many runs. Let me try a higher fallback probability.

Also: try p=1e-4 return from stop (even lower than 2e-4).

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 7:**

The champion (2394.71) still holds from round 4. Interesting findings:
1. GoToColor(red)/B=8 consistently scores ~2520-2590, slightly worse than champion's GoAwayColor(red)/B=9
2. Higher fallback probabilities (0.005, 0.01) are clearly bad (3164-3840)
3. Very low return-from-stop probability (1e-4) seems slightly better than (2e-4) for GoToColor(red) variant
4. Double green-detection transitions don't clearly help

**Why is the champion still winning?** GoAwayColor(red) keeps robots moving away from red walls → robots tend to stay in the center area, moving around. GoToColor(red) pushes robots toward walls. In a small arena (r=0.9m) with 10 robots, GoAwayColor might give better coverage/movement patterns.

**The persistent gap:** The champion at 2394 vs theoretical minimum. The issue is robots that can't perceive the green block. In an 8-wall octagon with r=0.9m, one wall turns green. The camera range is 0.45m. If a robot is >0.45m from the green wall, it can't see it. With inner radius 0.9m and diameter 1.8m, robots on the far side of the arena from green won't see it.

**New ideas to try:**
1. Try making the fallback from moving→stop even smaller (1e-4 or even 5e-5) - but won't help much
2. Try a 3-state machine: GoAwayRed → (on green signal) → GoToGreen (move toward green wall for a few steps) → Stop. But robots already stopped before reaching green...
3. Try combining B=9 (GoAwayRed) with color 2 (green) - but green isn't present until phase 2
4. Try different color targets for the moving state
5. Consider: what if moving state uses B=5 (repulsion) to spread robots to walls where they'd be closer to green?

Actually, let me reconsider: GoAwayColor(red) moves robots AWAY from red walls = toward center. GoToColor(red) moves toward red walls. Since green appears on a wall, robots near walls (GoToColor red) should be closer to green. But GoToColor(red) scored worse...

Maybe the issue is that GoToColor(red) clusters all robots near ONE red wall (the nearest), so they're all on one side and miss the green on the opposite side. GoAwayColor(red) keeps them moving through the center, giving more coverage.

**Unexplored territory:** What about using color 3, 4, 5, or 6 for the moving behavior? The walls are initially red (color 1). Or try a combination state that uses both behaviors.

Let me try: vary the moving behavior color (maybe the walls aren't all pure red?), try repulsion from neighbors to spread robots, and fine-tune the champion parameters.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 8:**

The champion (2394.71) still holds. Best performers this round:
1. p=1e-4 return from stop (vs champion's 2e-4) scores 2431 - close but not better
2. Double green detection in state 0 (redundant C=7 transitions) scores 2449
3. p=0.97 for green detection scores 2448
4. Stop state with "red wall detection → return to moving" (C=7, color 1) at low prob scores 2431

**Key insight:** The scores are very tightly clustered (2394-2483). The champion uses p=2e-4 return from stop, while p=1e-4 gives 2431. The difference might just be noise.

**What's the irreducible floor?** Looking at the score structure:
- The champion achieves 2394.71 averaged over many runs
- With 10 robots, 1200 steps, and a 400-600 step phase 1, perfect behavior would give ~0 score
- But robots far from the green wall rely on fallback (p=0.0005) → avg 2000 steps to stop
- This is the main cost: robots that don't perceive green

**Critical analysis:** If a robot is on the far side of the arena from the green wall (distance > 0.45m range), it never fires C=7(green). It must wait for the p=0.0005 fallback. Average wait = 1/0.0005 = 2000 steps. But phase 2 only lasts ~700 steps. So a blind robot costs 700 steps × 10 robot-weight = 7 per run per blind robot.

With 8 walls and 10 robots spread out, perhaps 3-4 robots are "blind" to green at any given moment. But robots are moving with GoAwayColor(red), so they cycle around the arena. Over 700 steps, a moving robot will likely pass near many walls. The question is whether the movement brings them close enough to the green wall.

**New approach:** What if we increase the fallback probability slightly (to 0.001 or 0.002) to help blind robots stop faster, accepting the small phase-1 cost? The champion uses 0.0005 which was chosen based on performance. But the worst performers showed that 0.005+ is bad.

Actually, 0.001 = 1000 steps average wait → might be better than 0.0005 = 2000 steps for blind robots, since phase 2 only lasts 700 steps anyway (so blind robots with 0.0005 stop randomly during phase 2 anyway - average at step 350 of phase 2 = 350 extra steps of movement).

With fallback=0.001: blind robots stop at average step 500 of phase 2 → 500 steps of cost
With fallback=0.0005: blind robots stop at average step 1000 of phase 2 → but capped at 700 → actually expected stopping time = min(geometric, 700) which for p=0.0005 gives expected stopping time ≈ 500 steps (since the geometric distribution with small p is roughly uniform in the early range)

Actually for blind robots, the expected additional cost with fallback p:
E[min(T,700)] where T~Geom(p)
- p=0.0005: E[min(T,700)] ≈ 700*(1-(1-p)^700) + sum... ≈ roughly 400 steps
- p=0.001: E[min(T,700)] ≈ roughly 300 steps
- p=0.002: roughly 200 steps

So higher fallback helps blind robots but hurts robots that do see green (false early stops in phase 1: p×500×10 = cost).

**Optimal tradeoff:** Let me try fallback around 0.001-0.002 to see if it beats the champion.

Also try: completely eliminate the fallback (p=0 impossible, but use p=1e-5) to minimize false stops, accepting that blind robots will always move for full phase 2.

## Reasoning

**Final round analysis:**

The champion (2394.71) has held for 6 rounds. This is the last round, so I need to maximize the chance of beating it.

**Key observations across all rounds:**
1. The champion: GoAwayColor(red) + C=7(green, p=0.95) → Stop + tiny fallback (p=0.0005) → Stop; Stop with C=5(p=0.0002) → moving
2. Best performers this round scored ~2517-2526, all worse than champion
3. Higher fallback (0.001) in moving state with p=1e-4 return from stop scores ~2517-2521
4. The champion uses p=0.0005 fallback (not 0.001) and p=2e-4 return
5. Interestingly, the champion is consistently the best over many rounds - it seems to be near the true optimum for this topology

**What hasn't been tried:**
- Very fine-tuned parameters around the champion: p=0.95 green detection, p=0.0005 fallback, p=2e-4 return
- Try p=0.0003 fallback (between 0.0002 and 0.0005)
- Try p=0.00015 return from stop
- Try p=0.0004 fallback
- The champion might just be optimal for this 2-state topology

**The score of 2394 is robust.** The "worst" performer this round (2555) was a copy of the champion with same parameters - just noise! The champion score of 2394 vs round 9 copy of 2555 shows high variance. We should submit multiple copies of the champion to ensure it's re-evaluated and potentially beat itself.

Actually wait - the optimiser keeps track of best-ever score. Multiple copies of champion won't help if we're trying to find something better.

**Final strategy:**
1. Keep champion (elitism)
2. Fine-tune around champion parameters
3. Try a few genuinely different approaches that haven't been tried

The key parameter space to explore:
- Green detection probability: 0.90, 0.92, 0.95 (champion), 0.97, 0.99
- Fallback moving→stop: 0.0002, 0.0003, 0.0004, 0.0005 (champion), 0.0006
- Return stop→moving: 0.0001, 0.00015, 0.0002 (champion), 0.0003

The champion uses a specific combination. Let me try nearby combinations systematically.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
OBJECTIVE. This mission has TWO SUCCESSIVE PHASES separated by a signal, and the scoring rule is REVERSED between them. The score is MINIMISED.

PHASE 1 - BEFORE THE SIGNAL. At the start of each run the loop function draws a secret switch time uniformly between 400 and 600 simulation steps. Until that step, the score gains +1 for every robot that is STATIONARY. During phase 1 the robots must KEEP MOVING.

PHASE 2 - AFTER THE SIGNAL. Exactly at the switch time, one randomly chosen wall block of the arena turns GREEN. From that step on the rule flips: the score gains +1 for every robot that is MOVING. The robots must STOP and stay still for the rest of the run.

A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

CONSEQUENCE FOR THE FSM. Stopping immediately is a BAD strategy: it costs about one point per robot for each of the ~500 steps of phase 1. Moving for the whole run is equally bad, for the ~700 steps of phase 2. The machine needs at least one moving state and one stopped state, and the transition into the stopped state must be driven by PERCEIVING THE GREEN BLOCK. A transition based only on a fixed probability cannot be accurate, because the switch time is redrawn at every run. The floor is uniformly GRAY, so floor conditions carry no information in this mission.

# 2. THE ARENA FOR THIS RUN
The arena is a regular polygon with 8 walls, inner radius 0.90 m. 10 e-puck robots start scattered inside a disc of radius 0.56 m around the centre, with random orientations. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.45 m: neighbour-count conditions only see robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).
For reference, the published setup uses 20 robots, an inner radius of 0.91 m and a range of 0.50 m, so thresholds tuned for that setup may need adjusting here.

# 3. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 4. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 5. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 6. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
